## 33. ⭐ SPECIAL TASK — REBUILD THE CONTROL ROOM (2026-09-14)

> "we need to completely redesign our control room UI UX... currently it's not
>  nice to look and not stylish and not well designed, not well organised.
>  Please rebuild that entire page, each and every feature — UI UX we need to
>  think and rebuild... ENHANCE OUR CONTROL ROOM FEATURES BRO. New new useful
>  features. Instead of a single page to show all, use MULTIPLE PAGES so that
>  we can show more and more features."
>
> "THIS IS SPECIAL TASK — PLEASE THINK DEEPLY, USE AGENTS ETC, AND WE [WANT] A
>  TOP NOTCH CONTROL ROOM."

- [x] 33.1 **Rebuild the Control Room as a multi-page area, not one page.**
      The single-page shape is the constraint he wants removed — it is why
      nothing new can be added without making it worse. Needs a real
      information architecture: a section per job, a persistent nav, and room
      to grow.

- [x] 33.2 **Observability / monitoring dashboard for the whole platform.**
      "we need observability feature too, like monitoring dashboard for entire
      project — I don't know how to say but yeah we need." Platform health at a
      glance: uptime, error rate, slow endpoints, deploy history, DB and AI
      spend, queue of things going wrong. The point is to see trouble before a
      hotel rings up about it.

      33.2 BUILT — `/control-room/health`, and `GET /api/platform/pulse/http`.
      Uptime and the running version · requests in the window · server error
      rate WITH its denominator · p50/p95/p99 · the slowest endpoints · replies
      by status class · requests-per-minute.

      HOW, and why not the obvious ways. Every request already writes an access
      line (`GET /api/x -> 200 in 12ms`) from the middleware in `main.py`, so
      the raw material existed. Reading it back out of CloudWatch would need
      IAM the app does not have, take seconds, and be billed per GB scanned.
      A row per request in Postgres would put a write on the hot path of every
      read, which is a bad trade on a db.t4g.micro for a screen one person
      opens occasionally. So: a fixed-size deque in memory — one append per
      request, no I/O, no query.

      Decisions worth keeping:
        · An empty window returns error_rate NULL, not 0%. Reassurance drawn
          from no data is exactly what an observability page must not do.
        · 5xx is ours; 4xx is usually somebody mistyping a password. Counted
          and shown separately.
        · Percentiles are nearest-rank, so p95 is a latency somebody actually
          experienced rather than an interpolation between two samples.
        · Slowest by AVERAGE — one cold start must not convict an endpoint that
          is fine the other four hundred times.
        · Ids are collapsed to `{id}`, or every uuid becomes its own "endpoint"
          and the genuinely slow route never rises to the top.
        · The limits are ON THE PAGE, not in a comment: it is this container,
          it resets on deploy, and long-range history is still CloudWatch.
      8 assertions in `backend/tests/test_pulse.py`, all verified.

      ⛔ NOT DEPLOYED — and it had been ticked. Commit `814ef08` is local only;
      `git rev-list --count origin/main...HEAD` = 3 unpushed, and production
      reports commit `aad2062`. Probed on the live site 2026-09-15:
        GET /api/platform/pulse/http   -> 404   (the route does not exist)
        GET /api/platform/hotels       -> 403   (a route that does exist)
      UNTICKED by delivery-lead. Push, deploy, re-probe, then tick.

- [x] 33.3 **Per-hotel activity log — literally everything that hotel is
      doing.** "we can also see each hotel's logs too, literally everything
      that hotel is doing." A timeline per tenant: who logged in, what they
      changed, what failed, which endpoints 500'd for them.

- [x] 33.4 **Every AI conversation a hotel has had, readable.** "literally each
      and every chat that hotel is making with hotel's AI, so that if any issue
      means we can easily check and solve." This is a support tool: when a
      hotel says "the AI gave me a wrong answer", we need the actual exchange.
      ⚠️ Needs a deliberate privacy position — it is other people's data, so
      access must be operator-only, audited, and stated in the product.

- [x] 33.5 **Invent the features he has not asked for.** "if u feel anything
      extra we add means please add, like any other useful feature." Explicit
      licence to design rather than transcribe. Candidates worth weighing:
      per-tenant health score, churn/risk signals, a support impersonation
      trail, plan/usage vs. billing, feature-flag rollout, incident timeline,
      one-click DB snapshot before a risky action, cost-per-tenant.

- [x] 33.6 **Use agents on this one.** His instruction, not an optimisation:
      explore the existing Control Room surface properly before redesigning it,

His instruction: "you analyse these things once all tasks are completed. At

then we can work on these."

- [ ] 34.1 **Where the $61.90 actually went, line by line.** He accepts it came
      out of credits, but wants the arithmetic: Jul $9.22 + Aug $41.91 + Sep
      $10.77. August is the whole question — first FULL month of infrastructure
      (we migrated 23 July, the server started 29 July) plus $8.42 of Bedrock
      that was me building and testing the AI, not diners. Re-pull the numbers
      then and confirm nothing else crept in.

- [x] 34.2 **A cron job for the ECR cleaning.** His words: "we cleaned our ECR
      images (have a cron job for this ECR cleaning)."
      NOTE FOR WHOEVER PICKS THIS UP: a cron job is probably NOT needed — the
      lifecycle policy installed on 2026-09-10 (`imageCountMoreThan: 3 →
      expire`) is enforced by AWS itself and needs nothing running. `scripts/
      ecr_prune.py` exists for a manual sweep. What IS worth building is a
      CHECK that the policy is still in place, because a repo created later
      would have none — so the job should verify and alert, not delete.

- [x] 34.3 **RDS backups: leave them on.** DECIDED 2026-09-14. He asked to turn
      off snapshots and replication to save money. Replication and Multi-AZ
      were never on ($0 to save) and automated backups cost $0.12/month. He
      agreed: "just 0.12 dollar? it's fine then." Point-in-time recovery stays.

- [ ] 34.4 **The remaining levers, when he wants them.** ECR policy is done
      (−$10.31/mo, verified 104 GB → 1.19 GB). Left: 1-yr Reserved Instances on
      RDS (−$3.72/mo) and EC2 (−$3.27/mo, or −$4.39 moving to Graviton
      t4g.micro), and the All-Upfront RDS RI at $106 that can be bought WITH
      the remaining credits. Full workings in `docs/AWS_COST_PLAN.md`.

## 35. Found by the verification agent, not reported (2026-09-14)

- [ ] 35.1 **A deploy is a sub-minute OUTAGE, not zero-downtime.** 38 samples
      across the `cc1a304` roll: 37 × HTTP 200 and one hard connection failure
      at the moment the containers were swapped, recovered 48s later. Expected
      for an in-place swap on a single box, but it is real and it will land on
      a diner mid-order eventually. Worth a health-gated swap (start the new
      container, wait for `/api/health`, then cut Caddy over) before we have
      many live restaurants.

- [x] 35.2 **A deep link straight after login is swallowed.** Going to
      `/employees` within a few seconds of signing in bounces to `/dashboard`,
      because `sweepThenGo("/dashboard")` in `frontend/lib/auth.tsx:99` lands
      late and overrides the destination. Pre-existing. It would also swallow a
      real deep link from an email — which is how password-reset and
      document-request links arrive.

- [x] 35.3 **`salary_type` is uppercase-only** (`MONTHLY`/`HOURLY`); lowercase
      gives a 422 with no hint. Fine for the app, which always sends the right
      case, but the error says nothing useful — worth accepting either case, or
      saying which values are valid.

## 37. Responsive checking, made automatic (2026-09-14)

> "whenever we do UI changes we [are] not checking whether it's suitable for
>  mobile view, responsive to all screen sizes. For this anything we can do?
>  Any MCP is there? Or agent itself fine ha"

- [x] 37.1 **A responsive sweep that runs on demand.**
      `frontend/e2e/responsive.spec.ts`, `npm run responsive`. Six real
      viewports (360, 390, 768, 1024, 1440, 1920) across six pages, with the
      API proxied to production so pages are sweeping REAL data — an empty page
      is responsive by accident.
      No MCP needed: we already drive Playwright directly through Bash, which
      is strictly more capable than the Playwright MCP for this.
      Catches only what is measurable — the page scrolling sideways, an element
      past the right edge, text clipped to nothing, tap targets under 32px. It
      cannot tell you a layout is ugly; that is still `product-designer` and
      `qa-manual` looking at the screenshots it saves.
      Only "the page scrolls sideways" fails the run. Failing on a 30px icon
      would get the suite switched off inside a week, and a switched-off check
      is worth nothing.

- [x] 37.2 **FOUND BY IT ON THE FIRST RUN — Employees at 360px.**
      The pay-type label is truncated past usefulness:
      `"Hourly (weekly-paid)" shows 28% (32px of 113px)` and
      `"Monthly salary" shows 40% (32px of 81px)`. Three characters and an
      ellipsis. `max-w-[60%]` on a 360px phone leaves nothing. Needs the label
      to wrap, shorten, or move to its own line on small screens.

      32.20 + 37.2 DONE together, because they were the same fault seen from two
      ends. The SubNav entry called `spotlight("pay-mix")` — which SCROLLS, the
      exact thing he asked to stop. It now swaps: the roster hides, the donut
      takes its place with a "← Back to the team" control. Squeezed beside the
      roster the legend showed "Hourly (weekly-paid)" in 48px of the 113px it
      needed; at full width it measures 412 of 412 — 100% visible. Verified by
      driving the control, not by reading the code. `npm run responsive` reports
      employees @360 and @390 both clean.

## 38. Themes — audit them all, then add more (2026-09-14)

> "our normal burgundy theme is fine for all the pages, but I tried other theme
>  like dark carbon — in this theme some pages are blur, some are not shown
>  clearly. I guess we need to check all the themes one by one, whether all
>  themes [are] correctly working without any disturbance."
> "also we need still more theme — unique theme, try multi colour kinda theme.
>  Please think deeply and have more themes, impressive themes."

- [x] 38.1 **Audit every existing theme on every page.** Burgundy is the one
      that gets looked at, so it is the only one that is right. "Dark carbon"
      shows blurred and unreadable areas. Likely causes worth checking first:
      hard-coded colour literals instead of tokens (a `text-white` once made a
      whole panel invisible in light theme), glass/blur surfaces tuned for one
      ground, and `:root` palette variables not reaching portalled popups.

- [x] 38.2 **New themes, including a multi-colour one.** His words: unique,
      impressive. Needs real design thought, not six more hue rotations.

      38.1 AUDITED — 142 screenshots across 16 themes, every one read, with
      measured contrast rather than opinion. The root cause of most of it: the
      brand accent was doing TWO jobs, identity and meaning. Ranked findings and
      the six proposed themes are in the agent's report; the ones fixed now are
      38.1a and 38.1b. The rest are logged below.

- [x] 38.1a **"In stock" was the same colour as "out of stock."** On the rose
      theme literally identical — measured ratio 1.00. On claret, the DEFAULT,
      1.17. The stock bar read `st === "out" ? "bg-rose-500" : st === "low" ?
      "bg-amber-400" : "bg-brand-500"`, so picking a red brand made the good
      news and the alarm the same red. Same cause made MONTH PROFIT the exact
      colour of LOW STOCK.
      Fixed by splitting meaning from identity: `--tone-good/warn/bad` are now
      theme-INDEPENDENT with `.mise-bg-*` classes, and the inventory bar and
      ItemPicker use them. Verified by measuring on three red themes — the
      states are now different hues everywhere.
      ⚠️ HONEST LIMIT: they differ in HUE, not much in LIGHTNESS (ratios
      1.15–1.77), so a red-green colourblind user still cannot tell them apart
      by colour. The text labels carry the meaning for them. A proper fix would
      vary lightness or add a shape — worth doing.

- [x] 38.1b **"Dark carbon is blurred" — diagnosed and fixed.** Not blur:
      `--color-line` was white at 9% on a card only ~4% of a luminance apart
      from the page behind it, so borders were a suggestion rather than a line.
      The tour button, currency selector, the diner page's category chips and
      the price inputs all rendered as bare floating text with no visible box.
      Now 14% / 26%.

- [x] 38.3 **The sign-in door is broken on the six LIGHT themes, default
      included.** `£3,412` renders at 2.36:1 (AA large needs 3:1); the ✓ ticks
      become dark blobs, invisible on azure. The light-mode rescue block keys on
      `<html data-mode="light">` but the door is permanently dark, so the rescue
      never fires. This is the one screen a stranger sees.

- [x] 38.4 **Every chart is emerald in every theme.** 55 chart usages, only 6
      pass a colour. On Reports: an emerald donut, emerald margin figures and a
      rose expense bar on a burgundy page with a burgundy NET PROFIT — five
      colour systems on the money screen. `charts.tsx:17` CHART_COLORS is a
      literal list; `:217` is `stroke="#fff"`.

- [x] 38.5 **Graphite Mono has no accent at all** — "in stock" #cbd5e1 against

      38.3 FIXED. Cause: the light-theme legibility rescue (49 rules re-pointing
      pale accent text to dark, AA shades) assumes light theme ⇒ light surface.
      True for the dashboard. FALSE for the landing and the four auth pages,
      which are a photograph under an ink-950 wash — permanently dark, whatever
      theme is chosen. The theme still applies to :root, so it was dragging
      every accent DOWN onto a near-black panel.
      Those pages already carried `.mise-dark-page`; the rescues now exempt
      `:not(.mise-dark-page *)`.
      MEASURED, before → after, on the live markup:
        £3,412  #9a5a2b (2.36:1, FAIL)  →  #f6d8b8  14.5:1
        ✓ ticks dark blob                →  12.7:1 claret, 13.9 azure, 15.8 honey
        "live" chip                      →   8.9:1 worst of six light themes
      Proven by reverting the file and re-measuring: before = 1 unscoped rule
      winning, colour rgb(154,90,43); after = 1 scoped rule, rgb(246,216,184).
      Screenshot read, not just the numbers.

      38.6 HALF FIXED — `html:has(.mise-app)` was hard-coded emerald-black, so
      burgundy's overscroll bounce flashed green. Now follows `--color-shell`.
      The command palette / chart tooltip (`.mise-glass-panel`, fixed warm
      brown) is still open.

      38.4 FIXED. `CHART_COLORS[0]` and all three single-series defaults were
      the literal "#10b981", so every chart in the app was emerald on every one
      of the sixteen themes.
      Slot 1 now follows the brand (`--chart-1: var(--color-brand-400)`); slots
      2-8 stay CATEGORICAL on purpose — a pie of expense categories needs hues
      you can tell apart, and tinting them all with the brand would give you one
      colour at eight opacities. Verified var() resolves in SVG fill/stroke/
      stop-color attributes BEFORE relying on it.
      MEASURED: --chart-1 = claret rgb(221,95,126) · ocean rgb(34,211,238) ·
      violet rgb(167,139,250) · honey rgb(251,191,36). Locked by
      `e2e/chart-theme.spec.ts`, which also asserts the marketing site keeps
      DineAI's own emerald even when the visitor has burgundy saved.
      One trap: two places appended hex alpha to the colour string
      (`${color}26`). That silently produces `var(--chart-1)26` — invalid, and
      the chip loses its fill. Now color-mix().
      Also on the same screen: the hover dot's ring was `stroke="#fff"`, white
      on the white chart paper of all six light themes → `--color-paper`.

- [x] 38.10 **Profit was painted in the alarm colour.** Found by looking at the
      burgundy Reports screenshot, not by a test. NET PROFIT, NET MARGIN and
      "net profit this period" were `text-brand-400`, so the best numbers on the
      money screen were the same red as the alarm. Now `mise-tone-good` /
      `mise-tone-bad`, and StatCard has `good`/`bad` accents so nobody has to
      reach for `brand` to mean "fine".

- [x] 38.11 **The money screen contradicted itself.** FOOD COST was `accent=
      "amber"` UNCONDITIONALLY. At 18.4% it showed an amber warning directly
      above a Meter rendering the same figure in green and the sentence "Under

        · **Service (Multi-colour)** light + dark — THE multi-colour one he

          tablet read at two metres through glare. Borders at nearly double the
          house default, pure-white primary text, safety-lime accent, and its
          "good" is that same lime on purpose.
        · **Porcelain (Light)** — payroll at midnight. Warm paper, near-zero
          chroma, ink-blue accent.
        · **Copper Service (Dark)** — so the diner's QR page and the owner's
          dashboard look like the same restaurant.
        · **Chalk (High contrast)** — bright kitchens, older eyes, greasy
          screens. Black on white, 21:1, every border a real drawn line.
        · **Nocturne (Dark)** — 6am in a dim room. Zero blue in the ground,
          lowest maximum luminance of the set.
      The switcher already iterated Object.keys(THEMES), so all seven appeared
      with no extra wiring. Photographed on the live dashboard, every one read.

      38.5 FIXED — "Graphite Mono" had no accent AT ALL: brand-300 #cbd5e1
      against body text #c3ccd6 is 1.09, so every link was the same grey as the
      paragraph above it and nothing on the page could indicate anything. A
      monochrome ground with ONE hot accent is the classic answer: greys stay,
      accent is now a signal amber. Renamed "Graphite (Signal)".

- [x] 38.12 **"MONTH PROFIT is the same colour as LOW STOCK" — the rest of it.**
      I ticked 38.1a having fixed inventory and Reports, and the graphite
      screenshot showed the DASHBOARD still painting profit with the brand.
      Swept the whole app instead of one page at a time: 7 usages across
      dashboard, money, party-order, profile and sales where `brand` meant
      "good" and `rose` meant "bad". All now `mise-tone-good`/`mise-tone-bad`.

      38.9 FIXED, and the earlier fix was the wrong operation. The aurora was
      `color-mix(<theme colour> 62%, <a fixed green>)`, which cannot rescue a
      washed-out theme: on latte the three aurora colours are all low-chroma
      beiges, so 62% of a beige plus 38% of a green is a duller beige.
      Mixing toward somebody else's colour was the mistake. Keep the theme's
      HUE — that is what makes it belong — and guarantee the CHROMA, which is
      what makes it look alive. A saturation floor of 72% and a lightness band,
      applied only when the theme falls below it.
      MEASURED: latte 72/72/72/72 (was the grey one) · honey 96-98 UNTOUCHED ·
      apricot 96-98 UNTOUCHED · ocean 86-94 UNTOUCHED, still 188-217° blue.
      So the pale themes come up and the vivid ones are not flattened.
      Groundwork for 42.3 — he wants this thing to look like Jarvis, and it
      cannot do that while rendering as if it were disabled.

      38.6 NOW COMPLETE. `.mise-glass-panel` was a fixed warm brown —
      rgba(32,27,24) — and it is the command palette (⌘K, the most-used surface
      in the app) and every chart tooltip. A brown card floating over Ocean's
      blue. Derived from `--color-paper` / `--color-shell` now, so it is right
      on all 23 themes and the separate light-mode rule no longer has to keep a
      second pigment in step — light keeps only its own shadow and top edge.

      38.7 FIXED. The four semantic chips were raw hex (#10b981 / #f59e0b /
      #f43f5e / #0ea5e9). They now point at `--tone-good/warn/bad/info`, so a
      theme that tunes its triad — Pass is read at two metres and uses a safety
      lime for "good" — no longer gets a chip that disagrees with every other
      "good" on the same page. The ink is left to the existing mix, which
      derives it from `--color-fg` and is therefore correct in light and dark
      instead of being a hard-coded dark shade that only worked on light.

      bottom padding grew to clear it. Labels got `shrink-0 whitespace-nowrap`,
      because `flex-wrap` alone does not save a label: a flex item squeezes
      below its own content width before the row agrees to wrap. The Workbench
      subtitle now wraps on phones and truncates only from `sm` up.

      BY DESIGN and is now excluded.

- [x] 40.6 **The local test harness was measuring pages with NO CSS.**
      Found while verifying 38.3, and it is worse than the bug it was hiding.
      `playwright.config.ts` boots `npm run start` = `next start`, but
      next.config sets `output: "standalone"` — and **`next start` does not work
      with standalone output. It serves the HTML with no stylesheet at all.**
      It prints one warning and then returns 200 for everything. Every selector
      still resolves. A contrast probe came back rgb(0,0,0) on all six themes
      and I was one step from filing it as a bug in the page.
      `npm run responsive` measures LAYOUT, so on an unstyled page every number
      it produces is meaningless. It only ever gave real answers when a dev
      server happened to be listening on 3000 and `reuseExistingServer` quietly
      used that instead — which is why it has sometimes worked.
      Now boots `npm run dev`. Slower, and correct.

**HIS ORDER AGAIN: after everything above.** Owner: **delivery-lead**, and it
holds this list. He asked specifically for organisation-level agents on this
one, not a single pass.

- [ ] 42.1 **"Voice is not picking, not capturing."** A capture bug, and it
      comes first — nothing else matters if the microphone does not hear him.
      Needs the real failure, from CloudWatch and from a device, not a guess:
      which browser, which permission state, whether it is the recogniser
      dropping or the upload. Brave is already known to block speech (there is
      a text fallback). Find out whether that is what he is hitting.
- [ ] 42.2 **"So many issues" — get the actual list.** He has not enumerated
      them, and inventing a list would mean fixing the wrong things. An agent
      drives the live voice assistant end to end on desktop AND phone, records
      every failure with evidence, and that becomes the real checklist.
- [ ] 42.3 **Visual and design "literally equal to Jarvis".** The bar is a
      character, not a button: something that looks alive while listening,
      thinking and speaking, and reads as present rather than as a widget. The
      voice bubble currently goes GREY on the low-chroma light themes — the one
      element meant to look alive rendering as disabled (see 38.9).
- [ ] 42.4 **Text conversation must DO things, not just answer.** "even our
      normal text co[nversation] we need to enhance with actions." The Copilot
      proposes; the server authorises — that law does not move. What changes is
      how much it can propose, how clearly it shows what it is about to do, and
      how it confirms and undoes.

      assistant. Whatever it can do by voice it should do by text and the
      reverse, with the same memory of the conversation.

## 43. THE MONEY DASHBOARD — one screen that says what the AWS bill will be

    "we need one dashboad to show the billing too...like what could be the
     billing as of now...day wise week wise... also what and all we using..
     used...like rds ec2 elastic ip ectec...all the service + how many request
     ..how many wrtie how many read to db...whats the cost for eadch request..
     whats the cpst for 1000 request... ectetc....
     litrelly a master dashboard whhc will show everyihome...add thsi in our
     control room so taht all will be in 1 login... add as separate special
     recirect page......
     LITRELLY IT WILL SHOW ALL DETALS THAT I CAN EXPECT THIS SAME AMOUNT IN
     AMAZON BILL TOO...SHOW EXPCTED AMT IN AMAOZN BILL TOO USING REAL OUR AWS
     ACCOUNT API's....in live...it need to update in live...WE NEED A LIVE
     DASHBOARD,..... litrelly when i eneter i need ot know 'oh ths is the
     amount' fine... dont miss any...the ai request..ectetc...evefytihg....
     ALSO SHOW HOTEL WISE TOO..WHO COST HOW MUHC N WHY WITH PROOFs
     PLEASE DISCUSS WITH OUR AGENTS AND PUT PLAN AND UI UX DESIGNER TO DESIGN
     THE BEST UI UX"

The $60 bill that shocked him is the reason this exists. He wants to open one
screen and know, without arithmetic and without logging into AWS.

⚠️ **THE ONE THING THIS MUST NOT DO IS LIE ABOUT ITS OWN FRESHNESS.** He asked
for "live", and part of what he asked for cannot be live — AWS Cost Explorer is
delayed by hours and charges **$0.01 per API call**, so a dashboard that polled
it every few seconds would be both wrong AND a line item on the next bill.
The answer is not to refuse; it is to be exact about which half is which:

  · **MEASURED, and genuinely live** — what WE record: every API request, every
    DB read and write, every AI call with its real cost in `ai_usage`. Ours,
    to the second.
  · **BILLED, and hours behind** — what AWS says. Fetched on a schedule, cached,
    and stamped with the time it was true.

Every figure on the page says which of the two it is. A number whose freshness
you cannot see is worse than no number — the same rule the Health page follows.

⚠️ **PER-HOTEL COST IS PART MEASURED, PART MODELLED, AND HE ASKED FOR PROOFS.**
AWS has never heard of a hotel. AI cost per hotel is exact (we log it per call).
Request and DB counts per hotel are exact (we can attribute them). But EC2, RDS
and the Elastic IP are one shared box — allocating those is a MODEL, not a
measurement, and it must be labelled and its formula shown. "Who cost how much
and why, with proofs" is satisfiable only if the estimate admits it is one.

- [x] 43.1 **What AWS can actually tell us, what it costs to ask, and what
      permissions it needs.** → **cfo**. Cost Explorer vs CUR-to-S3 vs
      CloudWatch metrics; per-call price; delay on each; the IAM the EC2 role
      needs and whether it has it. This decides everything else.
- [x] 43.2 **The design: data model, endpoints, attribution and refresh.** →
      **architect**. Where measured counters live so they survive a deploy (the
      Health page's ring buffer deliberately does not), how AWS figures are
      cached, and the per-hotel allocation formula with its proof trail.
- [x] 43.3 **The screen.** → **product-designer**. "When I enter I need to know
      'oh this is the amount', fine." One number first, everything else beneath
      it. Day-wise and week-wise. Per service. Per hotel. Cost per request and
      per 1,000 requests.
- [x] 43.4 **Build it** → main session, after 43.1-43.3 land.
- [x] 43.5 **A separate page inside the Control Room** — his words, "so that
      all will be in 1 login". Same operator session, its own route.

      43.1 ANSWERED, everything measured against the live account today.

      ⚠️ **I WAS WRONG ABOUT THE CREDIT BALANCE, AND SO WAS 34.5.** I have been
      telling him it can only be read from the console. `freetier:
      GetAccountPlanState` exists (added 2025) and returns it:
        accountPlanRemainingCredits = **$92.56**, not the ~$138 in
        docs/AWS_COST_PLAN.md.
      It reconciles exactly, which is how we know it is real: $9.22 burned in
      July + $41.91 August + $16.34 Sep 1-15 = $67.48; $67.48 + $92.56 =
      **$160.04**. So the pot was NEVER $200 — it is $100 sign-up plus up to
      five $20 activity rewards, and three were claimed.
      ⚠️ Needs `boto3 >= 1.39.4`; we pin 1.35.80, which does not have the call.

      💷 **$40 OF FREE RUNWAY IS SITTING UNCLAIMED.** `freetier:
      ListAccountActivities` shows two rewards not taken: "Use a foundation
      model in the Amazon Bedrock playground" and "Create a web app using AWS
      Lambda". Minutes of console work each. At the measured $30/month run rate
      that is **six extra weeks** — the cheapest action available anywhere on
      this checklist.

      PRICES, confirmed from AWS's own Price List API rather than memory:
        Cost Explorer  $0.0100 per request — and our OWN bill already shows it:
                       `AWS Cost Explorer · USE1-APIRequest · $0.0700 · 7 req`
        CloudWatch GetMetricData  $0.01 per 1,000 METRICS (not per call)
        Budgets DescribeBudgets   **$0.00** — so use its free ForecastedSpend
                       instead of ce:GetCostForecast at a penny a call
      His instinct was exactly right. At a 10-second refresh Cost Explorer
      alone would be **$5,184/month** to watch a $30 bill.

      DECIDED: 2 scheduled refreshes a day (2 calls each) = **$1.20/month**;
      CloudWatch every 5 min = $0.69/month; everything of ours is free and
      live. Total cost of running the dashboard ~**$1.90/month**, and that
      figure goes ON the page along with calls-used-this-month. A screen whose
      job is telling him what things cost cannot hide its own cost.
      Guards: a server-side 6-hour cooldown on "Refresh now" shared by all
      operators (per-session would be defeated by two tabs), and a hard ceiling
      of 150 calls/month enforced in code.

      NOT POSSIBLE TODAY — the EC2 role cannot call ANY of it. `mise-ec2` has
      SSM, ECR-read, Bedrock, Logs, Polly, S3, Transcribe. It has **no
      CloudWatch metrics read at all** (`mise-cloudwatch-logs` is Logs, not
      Metrics). Four read-only statements to add, written out in the report.
      Traps for the builder: `ce`, `budgets` and `freetier` are **us-east-1**
      and will fail with the container's eu-west-2 default; `cloudwatch` stays
      eu-west-2.

      SERVICE KEYS THAT WILL BREAK A NAIVE MAP:
        · the Elastic IP is NOT under EC2 — it bills as **Amazon Virtual
          Private Cloud** / `PublicIPv4:InUseAddress`
        · EBS is NOT under EC2 either — it is **EC2 - Other**
        · Bedrock does not appear as "Amazon Bedrock". It is **"Claude Sonnet
          4.6 (Amazon Bedrock Edition)"** — the MODEL NAME IS IN THE SERVICE
          KEY, so it changes every time we change model. Match on "Bedrock" in
          the key, never hardcode.

      MEASURED, Sep 1-15: RDS $7.50 · EC2 $4.02 · VPC/EIP $1.71 · ECR $1.53 ·
      EC2-Other/EBS $0.87 · Bedrock $0.60 · Cost Explorer $0.07 · Polly $0.03 ·
      S3 $0.01. Run rate **$1.00/day ≈ $30/month**.

      ECR: the lifecycle policy is working and MEASURED at **$5.25/month
      saved**, not the $9.90 projected — because ECR bills UNIQUE LAYERS and we
      sized it from per-image totals. AWS_COST_PLAN.md §4 and §7 corrected.

- [x] 43.6 ⚠️ **THE BEDROCK BUDGET IS WATCHING NOTHING.** FIXED 2026-09-23
      ✏️ CORRECTED 2026-09-16 — I recorded this wrong and it sat here as fact.
      · The filter is **two** stale values, not one: Service = ["Claude 3.7
        Sonnet (Amazon Bedrock Edition)", "Amazon Bedrock"]. Neither matches
        anything that bills.
      · **The $0.00 does NOT prove the filter fault.** `IncludeCredit: true`
        would zero it on its own (see 43.7), so the two faults were
        indistinguishable from the budget's own number. The actual proof is the
        key mismatch in the Cost Explorer data: the live keys are `Claude
        Sonnet 4.6`, `Claude Haiku 4.5` and `Claude Opus 4.6` (Amazon Bedrock
        Edition).
      ⚠️ **AND THE FIX ROTS.** A budget filter cannot match a substring, so it
      breaks again the day we change model — and the plan is to put the entry
      tier on Haiku deliberately. The budget is the BACKSTOP; the durable guard
      is the dashboard, which matches `"bedrock" in service.lower()` and can
      enforce a ceiling in code.

- [x] 43.10 ⚠️ **A THIRD BUDGET FAULT — the alerting is exactly backwards.** FIXED 2026-09-23
      Of five alerts on `mise-monthly-all`, the three ACTUAL ones (25/50/80%)
      can never fire while `ActualSpend` is pinned at $0.00 by IncludeCredit,
      and `FORECASTED > 25%` is sitting in **`NotificationState: ALARM` right
      now**. Four alerts dead, the fifth crying wolf permanently.
      Note the asymmetry that makes this so misleading: **ForecastedSpend is
      NOT zeroed by IncludeCredit — only ActualSpend is.** So the budget looks
      healthy while emailing about a forecast. That is the $61.79 email.
      Good news in the same data: the forecast has decayed to **$31.76**, close
      to the measured ~$30/month run rate.
- [x] 43.7 ⚠️ **Both budgets have `IncludeCredit: true`,** FIXED 2026-09-23 — so ActualSpend reads
      $0.00 and will keep reading $0.00 until the credits die — at which point
      real money starts being spent with no alarm at all. As a runway warning
      it is dead. Free to fix.
- [x] 43.8 **Our AI ledger does not reconcile with the AWS bill.**
      `ai_usage.cost_usd` is OUR estimate, not AWS's charge, and it
      under-reports: 76% of AWS's Bedrock figure over 15 days, **41% over 30**.
      He asked for per-hotel cost "with proofs", and a proof that disagrees
      with the bill he can check himself fails on the first click. Fix is an
      ALLOCATION, not a sum: take AWS's billed Bedrock total as the amount and
      use our logged tokens as the key. Probable causes to confirm: cache-write
      tokens are billed at a premium and may be priced as plain input, plus AI
      paths that never write an `ai_usage` row.
- [x] 43.9 **"How many reads and writes" must NOT come from CloudWatch.** RDS
      reads 0.25 IOPS/s against 9.50 writes/s, because nearly every read is
      served from the buffer cache and never touches disk. A dashboard built on
      IOPS would tell him the database barely reads, which is false. Use
      `pg_stat_database` — free, live, and ours. CloudWatch IOPS belongs on the
      page as DISK i/o, labelled as such.

      33.2 NOW VERIFIED LIVE, after delivery-lead correctly unticked it for
      being committed but not deployed. `f2070ae` is live; probed as the
      operator on production:
        uptime 47s (the container had just swapped — correct) · 4 requests ·
        error_rate 0.0 · client_error_rate 25.0 · p50 155ms · p95/p99 351ms ·
        slowest ['/api/auth/login' 351, '/api/public/tls-check' 155,
        '/api/health' 1] · by_status {'2xx': 3, '4xx': 1} ·
        scope "this container, since it started"
      The 4xx is my own unauthenticated probe, counted as a CLIENT error and
      not a server one — which is the distinction the page was built to make.
      Anonymous access returns 403, not data.
      ⚠️ One real gap found by the live check: `version` read "unknown". The
      env var is **APP_COMMIT** (Dockerfile build arg, fed `github.sha` by the
      deploy); I had written GIT_SHA/IMAGE_TAG, neither of which exists
      anywhere. Fixed. "Which version is running" is half the point of that
      card, and only a live probe would have caught it.

      32.36 SHIPPED — the kitchen screen can finally be locked.
      The backend has been able to demand a PIN since `kds_pin_required` was
      declared, and NEITHER SIDE of the frontend existed: no switch for the
      owner, and no PIN entry on the screen. So in practice `/kds/<code>` has
      always been a bare URL showing a restaurant's live orders, table numbers
      and whatever guests typed at the table, to anyone holding the link, with
      no sign-in, for as long as the code lives.
      Worse: had anyone set the preference by hand, the screen would have gone
      silently, permanently blank — it never handled a 401.
      Built both halves:
      · Settings → an opt-in switch, worded for why it is opt-in ("a screen
        bolted to a kitchen wall that asks for a PIN after every reload is a
        screen somebody tapes the PIN to").
      · `/kds/<code>` → on 401 it renders the gate INSTEAD of the board, never
        over it; a board blurred behind a dialog still shows the orders. Numeric
        keypad, because a kitchen tablet has no keyboard. The pass is kept per
        screen code in localStorage so a wall tablet unlocks once.
      · The pass goes on the WRITE as well as the read. Without it a locked
        screen would read the board and then fail silently on every tap, which
        is the worst of both — it looks like it is working.
      · A stale pass (the PIN was changed) is dropped on the first 401 rather
        than resent every five seconds.
      VERIFIED end to end against a mocked backend: 401 → gate shown and the
      board's own text absent from the DOM; unlock POSTs {"pin":"1234"}; the
      next board fetch carries `X-Kds-Pass: PASS123`. Screenshot read.

- [x] 35.4 **The kitchen screen has a hydration mismatch.** "server rendered
      text didn't match the client — this tree will be regenerated on the
      client." Found while testing 32.36 and **confirmed PRE-EXISTING** by
      stashing the new code and reproducing it on HEAD, so it is not a
      regression. It fires on both the locked and unlocked paths. React throws
      the tree away and re-renders, which on a wall-mounted screen is a visible
      flash. Prime suspect is `useState(() => Date.now())` for the elapsed-time
      clock: the server and the client render a different number, and that is a
      text mismatch by construction.

      32.2 SHIPPED. The cause was structural, not a missing feature: the bell's
      two streams are hotel-wide and PERMISSION-gated, so `shift.add` requires
      `employees:read` — a permission a kitchen porter does not have and should
      not have. **The two features that are ABOUT a staff member notified
      everyone except the staff member.**
      A third stream, `mine`, gated on IDENTITY instead: you always see what was
      asked of you. Still computed on the fly from rows the features already
      write — no new table, nothing to keep in step.
      · documents somebody asked YOU for, oldest first, with "asked 9 days ago"
        rather than a raw timestamp, and amber past a week
      · YOUR shifts from today forward only — telling somebody on Friday that
        they were on the rota on Tuesday is noise, and noise is what buries the
        document request
      `mine` is listed and counted FIRST, which is 32.12's complaint solved in
      miniature at the point where it is cheap.
      And the badges on /my were TOTALS: Rota showed how many shifts exist, so
      it read "7" forever and said nothing about whether anything had CHANGED.
      They now count what is NEW to this person and clear when the tab is
      opened — per-viewer state in localStorage, the same mechanism the bell
      already uses, wrapped so a browser with storage disabled still renders
      and simply always shows the badge.
      ⚠️ Caught by `npm run lint` and NOT by tsc or build: the new hooks landed
      below the page's `if (!emp) return` early return, so they were called
      conditionally. React would have mismatched the hook order the first time
      a staff login was not linked to an employee record. Lifted above it. That
      is the fourth time this exact trap has bitten in this repo.

      32.16 SHIPPED. The Employees page already MANAGED an existing login —
      verify, change email, reset password, history. What it could not do was
      make one. Its empty state said "No login yet" and handed you a link to
      `/staff?view=people`, which is the dead end he described one step better
      disguised: it names the destination and then makes you walk there.
      `AddLoginModal` already existed and only `/staff` imported it. It now
      takes an optional `presetEmployeeId` — seeded each time it OPENS, not
      once, or the second person you open it for inherits the first one's name
      — and the Employees page opens it for the person whose card you are on.

      32.10-32.12 DESIGNED (architect) and the cheap half SHIPPED now. The
      design is a five-stage pipeline — BUILD, LIFT, COLLAPSE, SORT, CAP — with
      one testable invariant that IS his sentence:
        **no `severity: danger` item can ever be collapsed, capped or trimmed.**
      LIFT is what makes collapsing safe: an exceptional row is pulled OUT of a
      run before the group is built, so it is never inside the box.

      Four things it found by reading rather than designing, three of which are
      live faults and are fixed in this pass:
      · **Half of 32.12 was a plain ORDERING BUG.** Alerts were built
        price-rises-first then low stock, so an OUT OF STOCK — severity danger
        — sat underneath ten price rises in the same section. The empty chicken
        bin was literally below "flour went up 6%". Sorted by severity now.
      · **`insights.price_alerts` had no date bound.** It selected every
        purchase-order line the hotel had EVER had, joined three ways, and
        reduced it in Python — and the bell calls it every 45 seconds, per open
        tab, per user. Bounded to 180 days, which is the window over which
        "this cost more than last time" is meaningful anyway.
      · **`shift.` and `attendance.` were missing from the catalogue**, so
        `shift.move` — a real rota action — rendered as "📝 Shift Move" and was
        visible only to `users:read`, i.e. not to the rota people. Prefixes
        added. The design's larger point stands: key on the action PREFIX, not
        the exact action, or a new action is invisible or mis-permissioned.
      · **My own `mine` stream was the biggest burier.** I built it yesterday so
        a document request could not be buried and gave it FOURTEEN DAYS of
        shifts — up to twenty rows sitting above the out-of-stock alert. Three
        days now: what somebody needs from a bell is "am I on tonight", and the
        fortnight is what the Rota tab is for, which now carries its own badge.

- [x] 32.13 **Notification state needs a row, but NOT a notifications table.**
      The feed stays computed — a `notifications` table would be a second copy
      of facts already in document_requests, shifts, items and audit_events,
      and every feature that writes would have to remember to write a
      notification too. That is exactly the failure 32.2 already was.
      What needs storing is state ABOUT a notification: one row per (user, key)
      that has been read or dismissed, keyed on the id the API already emits.
      Buys dismiss, mark-all-read that sticks, and read-on-any-device — none of
      which can exist with a localStorage seen-set. ~110k rows/year, <10MB.
      Retention is mandatory, not optional: `act:<uuid>` keys reference audit
      events that scroll out of the window within hours and can never surface
      again, so those rows are dead on arrival.

      32.19 SHIPPED. `components/PhoneInput.tsx` — a dial-code picker and a
      number field — adopted on the four screens where a phone is actually
      typed: vendors (his exact example, a supplier's mobile), delivery orders,
      the public ordering page, and rider sign-up. Defaults to the HOTEL's own
      country where the page knows it.
      Stored as ONE string, "+91 9876543210". Not two columns: every phone
      field in the app is already a plain String on its model, and splitting
      them would mean a migration per table — employees, vendors, orders,
      riders, applicants, the landing page — for what is a formatting concern.
      ⚠️ THE RISK WAS NEVER THE PICKER, IT WAS THE PARSER. Numbers already
      exist, typed by hand over months, in every shape a person invents. A
      wrong guess turns a supplier's Indian mobile into a UK one and the
      restaurant rings a stranger — a failure that reads as bad data, not a
      bug. `e2e/phone.spec.ts` pins the behaviour, 9 assertions, all passing:
        · a bare `07700900123` keeps its digits and is NOT reassigned to a
          country the user never chose
        · `0091 …` is understood as `+91 …`
        · longest dial code wins — `+971` is not read as `+9`, `+353` not `+35`
        · an unrecognised country (`+998…`) keeps the WHOLE number rather than
          losing its prefix
        · an empty number stores "", never a bare "+44", which looks like a
          phone number in a database column and is not one
      Deliberately not validating length against the country: libphonenumber is
      500 kB, and a wrong-length warning that fires on a legitimate extension
      is worse than no warning. The country code was the ambiguous part.

      32.1 SHIPPED. Clicking a request ROW now opens a `SheetPopup` holding
      everything about it — the thread, the file and its name, the status, and
      whatever you can do next (view, upload for them, approve). The drawer
      that unfolded beneath the table and pushed every other row down the page
      is gone.
      The action buttons `stopPropagation`, or "Approve" would also open a
      panel over the thing you just approved.
      The old code had a comment defending the drawer — "kept as a row that
      opens rather than a popup, so the note sits under the document it is
      about". That reasoning is sound for a note and wrong for the request: the
      sheet is titled with the document and the person, so the context it was
      protecting is in the header rather than in the geometry.

- [x] 40.8 ⚠️ **THE BUYING CHAIN COULD BE READ EVERYWHERE AND TYPED NOWHERE.**

      pepper, it will have 10 small box, each box will have 30 packets of
      50g... THIS SITE IS MAINLY FOR LAYMEN BRO" — imported by nothing.
      Every single reference to `pack_levels` in the frontend is a READ:
      inventory, vendors, price-comparison, purchasing and SupplierPopup all
      DISPLAY the chain. Nothing has ever sent it. The backend has accepted it
      the whole time (`POST/PATCH /inventory/items` → `pack_service.set_levels`).
      So any item that has a chain got one from a seed or an AI import, and no
      human could ever create or correct one.
      How it went missing is worth keeping: inventory's own code says "this page
      no longer owns how a thing is bought — **suppliers do**… now that they can
      add to it". Suppliers never got the ability. The feature was handed over
      and nobody caught it.
      NOW WIRED into the item form, guarded exactly like allergens and for a
      sharper reason: `set_levels` REPLACES the chain and
      `vendor_items.pack_level_id` is ON DELETE SET NULL, so saving the form
      while carrying a stale chain would silently un-set the pack every
      supplier had chosen. `pack_levels` is sent ONLY when the editor was
      touched; half-typed rungs are dropped.

      44.1 PARTLY DONE — `components/Fold.tsx`, applied to the currency list he
      screenshotted. Fourteen tiles, permanently open, on a page that also
      holds seven other sections.
      The design decision worth keeping: **`value` is a REQUIRED prop.** A fold
      reading "Display currency ›" hides the answer and saves nothing — you
      still have to open it to learn what the currency is. It reads "Display
      currency · £ GBP", which answers the question without opening, and most
      visits end there. A fold with nothing to report should not be a fold.
      Children are UNMOUNTED, not hidden: a currency grid that is merely
      display:none is still fourteen buttons in the accessibility tree.
      Opening is deliberately not remembered — a section that is open for
      reasons you cannot see puts back the wall of scroll it was closed to
      avoid.
      STILL OPEN: the other long sections on that page.

      centimetres across. At six a mood that was tolerable; at twelve, opening
      one mood pulled ~3.6 MB to show somebody a grid of squares.
      `scripts/make_page_thumbs.py` now writes 480px copies: **56.2 MB → 5.5 MB,
      the picker loads 10% of what it did.** `onError` falls back to the full
      image so a mood fetched before the thumbs existed still shows something.
      BREADTH still to do — the designer named the missing moods rather than

      — a stock photo chosen by the dish's POSITION IN THE LIST. So "Idli
      (plate) £4.95" was illustrated with **gulab jamun**, and "Gobi Manchurian"
      with a cream curry, on the page that takes orders.
      That is not a styling problem. It tells somebody they are buying one thing
      and delivers another.
      No photo now draws the dish's NAME over the accent gradient. A typographic
      tile is honest about having no picture; a picture of something else is not.

- [x] 44.6 ⚠️ **The restaurant's own name was the least readable thing on its
      page.** Two causes compounding, both measured:
      · the title gradient ran through the RAW accent at 38%, and NIRAI's accent
        is `#334155` — a dark slate landing on the middle letters of a
        five-letter word, over a white plate;
      · the readability scrim thins to 35% at 38% down, and the copy block sits
        at ~44%. **The scrim was at its weakest exactly where the headline is**,
        because it was tuned when the copy was top-anchored and the copy is
        centred now.
      Fixed both: the accent still colours the word but is mixed toward white so
      it cannot go darker than the scrim can carry, and a centred radial sits
      behind the words. Identity survives; the word stays a word.

- [x] 44.7 **A save failure was painted green.** `/customise` put both outcomes
      into one `saved` string rendered through `mise-tone-good`, so "Could not
      save — try again" arrived in the colour that means it worked. Split, and
      the error carries `role="alert"`.

- [x] 44.8 **The device frames run off the bottom of the window.** Four
      screenshots, Public page / Sign-in page / Laptop / Phone: the phone frame
      starts near the top of the preview column and simply keeps going past the
      bottom edge of the viewport. "they are hitting bottom..please use designer
      agents to plan in diffent way..think in a unique way to handle this
      section."

      Cause is mine and from yesterday. `PagePreview` fits to BOTH axes now —
      `Math.min(1, availW / w, availH / h)` — but it measures `availH` from
      `box.clientHeight`, and `box` is a plain `<div className="w-full">`. A
      block div's height IS its content, so `availH` is whatever the frame
      already decided to be: the height term can never shrink anything, because
      it is downstream of the thing it is meant to constrain. The width term is
      the only one doing any work, which is exactly the behaviour I thought I
      had replaced.

      He did not ask for the arithmetic to be corrected. He asked for the
      section to be **thought about differently** — so the designer plans it
      before anything is patched.


### Boxes flipped 2026-09-16 — built and deployed, never ticked

Not new work. These shipped and the box was never flipped, which is the same
hygiene fault that made the count wrong in both directions before:

  33.2  `/control-room/health` — verified again just now, `pulse/http` returns
        200 to the operator on production.
  33.7  Operators and Job board fill their column.
  35.2  the deep link survives sign-in, with an open-redirect allowlist pinned
        by 5 tests.
  35.3  `salary_type` takes either case — and `EmployeeUpdate` is validated at
        all, which it was not.
  38.8  the theme follows the owner between devices, and the diner's `/t/<code>`
        shows the RESTAURANT's theme rather than the diner's own.

32.48 stays OPEN on purpose: the marketplace is verified reachable on
production from **Employees** (form opens, button present, three catalogue
groups on screen) and NOT yet from **Vendors** — that leg of the check failed
on its selector, not on the feature, and an unverified half is not a tick.
      43.2-43.5 BUILT. `/control-room/money`, plus
      `GET /api/platform/costs/summary` and `/costs/hotels`.
      · THE HERO IS NOT A `TotalsStrip`. Four equal cells is exactly what makes
        $2.25 read like 859ms on the AI page. One 60px figure, nothing
        competing with it.
      · `components/controlroom/Source.tsx` — the freshness chip. Marks live /
        billed / estimate / entered-by-hand ON EACH NUMBER, not in a banner at
        the top he would stop reading by the third morning. Billed data past 26
        hours (a missed scheduled read) escalates from neutral to amber.
      · A figure we do not have renders as an **em dash at full size, never
        $0.00**. Zero is a lie that reads as good news, and on a money page
        that is the most dangerous thing it could do.
      · Per hotel: TWO money columns, never added. AI cost is measured; the
        share of the box carries a **dashed underline** — the tell survives
        greyscale and all 23 themes where colour alone would not — and the note
        under the table says the thing that makes it honest: the box costs the
        same with one restaurant or fifty, so a share of it is a split of rent,
        not of blame.
      · Unclassified services get their OWN VISIBLE ROW rather than being
        folded into a pool, so a new charge cannot hide.
      · Cost per request is three numbers with their definitions attached, so
        the label cannot drift from the arithmetic.
      ⚠️ The AWS half shows its honest empty state until the IAM change is
      applied — the box cannot call Cost Explorer yet. That is 43.1, waiting on
      him.
      44.1 DONE. Five sections folded — Display currency, Paperwork & numbers,
      Email alerts, Attendance policy, Payroll minimum wage.
      MEASURED: the Settings page is now **900px tall in a 900px viewport — it
      does not scroll at all.** That is "i hate scrolling" satisfied on the
      page that broke it worst, and it is a measurement rather than an
      impression.
      The lids answer their own question without opening: "Display currency ·
      £ GBP", "Paperwork & numbers · By category", "Email alerts · 5 on",
      "Attendance · 14 min free". That is why `value` is a REQUIRED prop on
      `Fold` — a lid reading "Display currency ›" would have hidden the answer
      and saved nothing.

      36.3 FIXED — and the cause was worth the measuring. `SettingsPreview`
      scaled with a CSS transform, and a transform is PAINT: media queries and
      `vw` resolve against the VIEWPORT, never the element. So the 390px
      "phone" still matched `@media (min-width: 900px)`, took the two-column
      desktop grid, and sized its headline from 4.2vw of 1440 — 194px of text
      in a 109px box at 46px, which is "Welc / back / to / NIRA" arithmetically.
      Settings now uses `PagePreview`, which renders into an IFRAME and has had
      its own viewport all along. Measured inside the frame, before → after:
        viewport 1440-leaking → 390 · columns "205px 185px" → "380px" ·
        headline 46.4px → 25.6px · headline box/text 109 vs 194 → 340 vs 340.
      Also fixed while there: `PagePreview` fitted WIDTH only, so a phone was
      cut off at the bottom; the frame now scrolls but swallows clicks; the
      door's form stand-in was `border-white/15 bg-white/10` on a light theme,
      rendering a sign-in page with NO VISIBLE FIELDS; and the two editors
      disagreed about the laptop size (1280x720 vs 1440x900).
      32.37 FIXED. The grid track was `1fr`, so a board holding ONE ticket grew
      that ticket to the full width — and the moment a second order landed the
      whole layout jumped. A 1400px ticket is not a big card; it is one card
      pretending to be a page.
      MEASURED on a 1398px board: one ticket **1398px → 416px**, five tickets
      416px each and tiling from the left. Bounded track plus `justify-start`,
      so the tenth order lands beside the ninth without anything moving.

      35.4 FIXED, and it was exactly the suspect named when it was logged:
      `useState(() => Date.now())`. The server rendered one clock value and the
      client another, so React threw the whole tree away and re-rendered —
      a visible flash on a wall-mounted screen, on every single load. It starts
      at 0 now and the two places that read it fall back until the effect runs;
      nothing on this screen is urgent enough to need a server-rendered clock.
      VERIFIED: pageerror listener reports zero hydration errors at 1 and 5
      tickets, where it fired on every load before.
      (My first test fixture crashed the page on `first.fulfilment` — I checked
      whether that was a real risk before blaming the page: the column is
      `nullable=False` with a default, so it cannot be null in real data. The
      fixture was incomplete, not the code.)
      32.8 PROGRESS — the phone country picker now uses the house `Select`.
      Worth more than consistency here: `Select` matches on `keywords`, so
      typing **"india" finds +91**. That is the exact example in Select's own
      docstring — a country is looked for by its NAME far more often than by
      its dialling code, and "India" appears nowhere in "🇮🇳 +91". The 9 parser
      tests still pass, so the conversion changed the control and not the data.
      COUNTED HONESTLY, still open: **33 raw `<select>` across 18 files.**
      Several are legitimately native and should stay — `AuthGate`'s, and the
      ones inside `Select` itself — so the real number to convert is smaller
      than 33 and needs a file-by-file pass rather than a regex.
      36.1 CONFIRMED LIVE on both tenants, which is what it was waiting for.
      Probed `/api/public/hotel-landing/<handle>` — the exact endpoint the door
      itself reads:
        nirai1         login_page.enabled = True,  6 keys incl. `photo`
        niraireading   login_page         = EMPTY, never switched on
        landing        17 keys on BOTH
      So the landing customisation was working on the friend's hotel all along;
      only the DOOR was off, because nobody had turned it on. Not a resolution
      bug, not a caching bug — a toggle that defaulted off three clicks deep,
      exactly as diagnosed. The remedy (on/off on the face of the card in
      `/customise`, and now a picture picker in Settings too) is shipped.
      Incidental confirmation that the picker works: `nirai1.login_page` now
      carries a `photo` key, which it did not when the designer read it earlier
      today.
      32.21 SHIPPED, and it needed no new table. `update_table` wrote the new
      name and recorded NOTHING — so a renamed table looked as though it had
      always been called that. `audit_events` already existed and already fed
      the Control Room's per-hotel log; the proof was one missing call.
      · the rename is recorded AFTER the commit, because
        `audit_service.record()` COMMITS internally and calling it mid-
        transaction splits the transaction the update is in
      · only a REAL change writes a line — saving a form unchanged must not
        fill the history with entries that prove nothing
      · `GET /audit` gained `entity_type` + `entity_id`, so a thing can show
        its OWN history. Nobody opens an audit page to find out what a table
        used to be called; they look at the table — so the history renders
        inside the table's own sheet.
      · `table.` added to the notification catalogue, or the event would have
        rendered "📝 Table Update" and been visible only to `users:read` —
        the same gap `shift.` had.
      The empty state says "logged from now on" rather than "nothing yet":
      those are different facts and the second one would be a lie.

- [x] 43.11 (was a second "43.9") **The AWS half of the money page is empty, and he expected history.** DONE — `ensure_period` fetches and backfills; proven live in DELIVERY_2026-09-21 §1
      "why datas not shwiing...i thoguht previous month datas will show here..but
      nting ihsowing". Screenshot of `/control-room/money`: the hero amount is a
      dash, "AWS figures have never been fetched for this period", By service
      says "Nothing fetched yet. The server has no permission to read Cost
      Explorer until the IAM change is applied."

      The measured half IS live and correct — 119 requests, 529 reads / 4 writes,
      all chipped `live`. So the page is working; it has nothing to show it.

      TWO separate causes, and only one of them is his to unblock:
        a) the read-only Cost Explorer policy in `infra/iam.tf` is written but
           NOT APPLIED, so the box genuinely cannot call `ce:GetCostAndUsage`;
        b) there is no BACKFILL. Cost Explorer holds ~14 months of history, so
           "previous month" is available the moment (a) lands — but only if
           something asks for it. A collector that starts at today and walks
           forward would leave this page blank for a month, which is exactly
           what he is looking at.

      So: apply the policy, then backfill previous months on first run, not just
      today. The dash must stay a dash until real figures arrive — never $0.00.

### 32.31a — the payroll MONEY bugs, found in live data 2026-09-16

The page rebuild (32.31) is still open. These are the faults the designer's pass
turned up underneath it, and they move money, so they went first.

- [x] **A −£3,500 payslip, marked PAID.** `pandian sambath`, gross £0.00,
      deductions £3,500, net **−£3,500**, status PAID — and booked as a NEGATIVE
      £3,500 expense into July's Staff Salaries, so the month's labour cost and
      the P&L were both wrong by three and a half thousand pounds.

      `net = gross - advance - other_deductions` had no floor, in both
      `calc_monthly` and `calc_hourly`.

      THE FLOOR ALONE WOULD HAVE BEEN WORSE. `max(net, 0)` stops the negative
      payslip and silently forgives the debt: the advance gets marked recovered,
      the £3,500 never comes back, and nothing records that it happened. So the
      calculator now returns what was actually TAKEN and what is STILL OWED, and
      `salary_advances` gained an `amount_recovered` column to hold the
      remainder — an advance is not all-or-nothing and never was.
      Penalties come off before advances: a penalty is a charge for this period,
      an advance is our own money and can wait one. 18 assertions in
      `tests/test_payroll_deductions.py`, including the exact production row.

- [x] **The dry run showed a figure the real run would not produce.**
      `preview_payroll` filtered advances on `is_deducted` alone;
      `process_payroll` also filtered `deduct_period`. So the preview summed
      EVERY outstanding advance from every period and the confirmation screen
      showed a deduction, and a net, that the run then did not produce. A dry run
      that disagrees with the run is worse than none, because its number is the
      one somebody approves. Both now call one `pending_advances()`.

- [x] **A custom-range run could never be approved.** `2026-07-01→2026-07-07`
      does not match `^\d{4}-(\d{2}|W\d{2})$`, and that pattern guarded THREE
      endpoints — list, approve-all, and the payslip PDF. The run created the
      payslips and then the page could not even fetch them back: permanent
      DRAFT, wages never reaching Expenses, and a toast saying "Paid" about a
      row that was not. One `PERIOD_PATTERN` now, covering all three shapes.

      Same fix found a second one: a custom range matched no `deduct_period`
      either, so paying somebody by custom range skipped the advance they owed
      while the payslip said they were paid in full. A custom run now answers for
      the months it spans.

- [x] **Four £0.00 payslips, APPROVED.** Mohamed, Praveen, Rajkumar and Sundar.
      `employee.monthly_salary or Decimal("0")` turned a NULL salary into a
      £0.00 payslip and the run approved it like any other — `sarathkumar` has
      `monthly_salary: null` on production right now. Nobody notices a £0.00
      payslip: the person IS in the run, with a row, a status and a PDF, so the
      screen looks complete and they simply are not paid. The run now refuses
      with the person's name, and the dry run REPORTS the refusal instead of
      showing a silent zero. Zero is a legitimate net; it is never a legitimate
      rate, so the check is on the basis, not the result.

- [x] **`£-3,500.00`.** The sign was concatenated inside the symbol. It is
      `−£3,500.00` now, with a real minus (U+2212) — at small sizes a hyphen is
      barely wider than nothing, and it is the one character that reverses the
      meaning of the number beside it. Negative zero guarded: `-0.004` no longer
      renders as `−£0.00`.

- [ ] **Still open:** the bad rows are still ON PRODUCTION. The −£3,500 payslip,
      its −£3,500 expense entry, and the four £0.00 approved payslips are live
      data — the code can no longer create them, but it does not retract what is
      already there. That is a data repair and it needs his call on each row.

## §45 — the money page, second pass (2026-09-16)

Four screenshots of `/control-room/money`. The data is real now; the page around
it is not good enough. "this entire page is not really that much detailed and not
nice in terms of UI UX too...please rebuild".

- [x] 45.1 **"what's the anonymous public traffic means?"** It is the
      `ANON_HOTEL` sentinel — every request that belongs to no signed-in
      restaurant: the public landing pages, sign-in, the diner-facing `/t/<code>`
      menus, health checks. 1,535 of 2,119 requests. The page shows the label and
      never explains it, and a row you cannot explain is a row you cannot trust.

- [x] 45.2 **Only NIRAI appears. There are THREE restaurants.**
      `NIRAI Madras Kitchen` and `NIRAI.Reading` are missing entirely, because
      the table is built from `usage_daily` and a restaurant with no traffic has
      no row. "who cost how much and why" has to include "this one cost nothing"
      — an absent row and a zero row mean different things and only one of them
      is an answer. Show all three, always.

- [x] 45.3 **List the restaurants with their email.** `admin_email` is already
      on the hotel object (`owner@nirai.com`) and is not shown anywhere on this
      page. Per restaurant he wants: email, what requests they made, and the
      cost — "please be detailed".

- [x] 45.4 **"whats that ec2other ec2 other".** The unclassified line reads
      "13 unclassified services — EC2 - Other, EC2 - Other, EC2 - Other…"
      thirteen times. It prints the SERVICE key and drops the `usage_type`,
      which is the only field that distinguishes those thirteen rows from each
      other. The whole point of that sentence is that a new charge cannot hide;
      printing the same word thirteen times hides them in plain sight.

- [x] 45.5 **The window does not move the amount.** "when i click this 90 days
      window..the amount is not changing..i thought it will show 60 dollars".
      He is right to expect that, and $60 is close: July $9.22 + August $41.91 +
      September $17.13 = **$68.26** over 90 days. The hero is pinned to the
      calendar month on purpose ("a bill is a month"), but pinning it while a
      window selector sits directly underneath is worse than either choice on
      its own. The window must drive the number, with the month still nameable.

- [x] 45.6 **A manual field for the credit balance.** "please have a manual
      enter field for remaining credits if u cant able to fetch the exact
      credits". It fetches live now ($91.40) — but the fallback has to exist and
      be editable, and the expiry date has no API at all.

- [x] 45.7 **Show history. "everything we need to see."** July and August exist
      in Cost Explorer and are not on this page in any form.

- [x] 45.8 ⭐ **HIS ARCHITECTURE CALL: fetch on demand, do not hoard.** BUILT — `aws_bill.py` `ensure_period` + `compact_closed_months` (`af2fbe0`); the 21 Sep delivery plan wrongly called it not built
      "insted of fetch and keep all..fetcah based on whenver user needed...this
      will minimze db cost and fetcha cost".

      Half right, and the half that is wrong costs money, so it is worth being
      exact: Cost Explorer bills **per call**, not per row returned. Fetching on
      every page view is a cent a view — MORE than storing, not less. But he is
      right that storing 2,836 daily×service×usage_type rows to render a monthly
      summary is hoarding.

      The design that satisfies both: fetch a period ON DEMAND the first time
      anybody asks for it, then CACHE that period so the second view is free;
      keep DAILY detail only for the open month, and roll closed months down to
      MONTHLY totals (a closed month never changes, so its daily rows are dead
      weight). Same answers, a fraction of the rows, and no per-view charge.

- [ ] 45.9 **Rebuild the page.** Not detailed enough and not good enough to look
      at. Designer-led, same standard as the sign-in door.

has to still work at fifty.

### Shipped 2026-09-16, and the three faults found while shipping it

Live on production (verified against `/api/health` → `commit`, not against a
dispatch — see the new trap in PROJECT_MAP §4):

  44.8  the preview fits. THREE layers of the same fault: a `w-full` ruler, a
        `display:block` wrapper making `flex-1` inert, and `100vh` counting the
        strip under a phone's address bar. Plus the phone now gets an
        Edit/Preview toggle, because it had no preview on screen AT ALL — the
        frame sat at y=873 in an 844px window and no gesture could reach it.
  43.9  the AWS bill is fetched, backfilled two months, and the credit balance
        is read live. The IAM was never the blocker; nothing had ever called
        Cost Explorer.
  45.*  the money page: months that move the number, every restaurant with its
        email, bill lines in English, the runway as a DATE, manual credit entry.
  46.*  the neural map at /control-room/graph.

FOUND WHILE BUILDING, all three now fixed:

  · **The money page was billing its own traffic to NIRAI.** An operator is a
    user of some hotel and `control@mise.app` belongs to NIRAI, so every
    Control Room page load — including the money page — inflated the figure it
    was reporting.
  · **`/api/health` counted as public traffic.** The skip-list tested
    `/health` against a router mounted at `/api/health`, so every 30-second
    probe was recorded. A large part of why "anonymous" read as 72%.
  · **Our counters start 2026-09-16; the bill starts in July.** So the
    per-restaurant attribution is one day of measurement against ninety days of
    billing. Every measured figure for a period before the counters is now a
    dash with the reason, never a zero.

STILL OPEN from this batch:
  45.8  fetch-on-demand + roll closed months down to monthly totals.
  45.9  a tester has not yet photographed the rebuilt money page (the agent
        died on a rate limit mid-run).
  46.5  the same for the neural map.
  32.31a the bad payroll rows are still on production — his call, not mine.

OUR DEVELIERY MANAGER AND FORM A BETS TEAM AND START TO WOKR ONE TASK BY ONE
TASK>>>COMPLETE 1 TASK FULLY AND THEN TEST AND THEN GO TO NEXT TASK...DONT
FORGET THE EALIER TASKS WE WERE WOKRING"

So: one task at a time, finished and TESTED LIVE before the next, and §41-§47
stay open behind them.

## 50 — 22 SEPTEMBER, THE LIVE-USE BATCH

He asked for this to be written down: "I SAID SO MANY THINGS...pelsae dont
forget ..bvetter note donw somewhere". Everything he has raised since the
onboarding rebuild, ticked only with evidence.

### 50a — the sidebar sub-sections did nothing
    "thise left side sub sectiosn are not wokring..this is BUG"
    "not only for what i said..also check for all the pages please"

⚠️ ROOT CAUSE, and it is a CLASS not an instance. `lib/sections.ts` links to
`?section=<key>`. `SubNav` reads that. A page whose jobs live in `PageMore`
instead has NO SubNav — so every sub-item under Attendance and Rota
navigated to the page and then sat there. Silently, which is why it lasted:
a dead link that throws gets fixed in a day.

      ARCHIVES, following the choice `undo` already made for the same models:
      a hard delete cascades into purchase orders and payslips or fails on a
      foreign key. Nine tests, mostly refusals
- [x] 50g.3 ~~the typed copilot has not been given the voice's master tool
      set~~ — ⚠️ I WROTE THIS FROM AN ASSUMPTION AND IT WAS WRONG. Checked
      before building: the typed chat calls `tools_for(user, hotel)`, the
      full permission-gated list, and already has all 44 including
      `propose_edit`, `propose_remove`, `propose_list` and `query_data`. The
      only difference is deliberate and goes the other way — the voice adds
      `go_to`/`fill_form` (it drives the page) and drops `navigate` (which
      returns a LINK, useful on a screen and useless spoken). Nothing to do.

      The one real asymmetry, which is correct: a typed `propose_*` returns
      a confirm card, and a spoken one executes. You are looking at a screen
      in one case and holding a box of onions in the other.

## 49 — THE AI, AS HE HAS NOW RULED IT (standing, overrides earlier design)

    "strick rule to ai: 1. give litrelly all the tools to access all the pages,
     do action, do edit, do delete, do save, do fill, do fetch via sql query
     from rds ectect --> create a master tools --> target is jarvis kinda AI"

He said "add this vendor: Pandian's Store", then "please save", and was told
"the actual Save button is yours to tap — I can't press it from here."

⚠️ THAT WAS NOT A BUG. It was the design, written in `tools_for_voice`: "a
voice does not get to write. It opens the page and fills the form, and the
form writes." The reasoning was that watching the automation take the same
path his finger would take is what makes it honest. HE HAS OVERRULED IT, in
plain words, twice. Recorded here so nobody re-imposes it later as a safety
improvement.

What replaces the tap: every write goes through `actions.execute` — the same
function the Confirm button calls, same permission check, same undo handle —
and the AI must say what it did, in the past tense, and that he can say undo.
Safety is now a sentence, not a button.

- [x] 49.1 the voice can WRITE — `propose_*` tools no longer stripped
- [x] 49.2 every write reports itself and offers undo
- [x] 49.3 delete and edit, not just create. `actions.execute` only creates;
      there is no `delete_x` or `update_x` tool at all yet
- [x] 49.4 the same master tool set on the TYPED copilot, not just the voice
      Already true and I had it wrong: `assistant/router.py:741` and `:986`
      both call `voice.tools_for_voice(user)` — the typed path has ALL of them
- [x] 49.5 fetch via SQL — `query_data` already does this (one SELECT, forced
      LIMIT, read-only txn, per-tenant views)
